**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops integral representations for generalized Rényi divergences on abstract von Neumann algebras. It achieves this by relating these divergences to integrals of the standard relative entropy using advanced tools from non-commutative functional analysis. The result provides a unified mathematical framework for quantifying quantum distinguishability in highly general quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep mathematical machinery connecting divergences to integrals over relative entropy is highly relevant for understanding quantum channel capacities and resource theories in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive integral representations for sandwiched and $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi divergences when defined on general von Neumann algebras.

**Main result.** It establishes that these divergences can be represented as integrals over ordinary relative entropies, providing a powerful mathematical tool for analyzing quantum distinguishability.

**Method.** The derivation employs advanced functional analysis tools, specifically Haagerup non-commutative $L^p$ spaces, interpolation, and fixed-ray constructions.

**Model / system.** The theory is formulated for normal states on arbitrary von Neumann algebras, generalizing concepts from finite-dimensional quantum information theory.

**Key observables.** Sandwiched and $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi divergences ($D_{\alpha,z}(\rho\|\sigma)$), and the ordinary relative entropy $D(\cdot\|\cdot)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi order $\alpha$, the parameter $z$, and the support inclusion condition between the states $\rho$ and $\sigma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption for the general case is the support inclusion condition ($	ext{s}(\rho) \le \text{s}(\sigma)$), though boundary terms are derived for when this fails.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the integral representation for finite dimensions, extending it to general von Neumann algebras using $L^p$ spaces, and finally detailing the fixed-ray construction to prove the general identity and analyze boundary corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We represent sandwiched and $α$-$z$ Rényi divergences as averages of ordinary relative entropy. The $α$-$z$ Rényi divergence is shown to be an integral over the relative entropy of a canonical family of fixed-ray escort states along the ray $z=cα$. We prove this representation for normal states on an arbitrary von Neumann algebra, using Haagerup non-commutative $L^p$ spaces and interpolation. The formula holds for every $z>0$: for $0<α<1$ it holds when the support of the first state is contained in that of the reference state, and for $α>1$ it holds whenever the divergence is finite. When the lower-order support condition fails, we identify the exact fixed-ray support-boundary term. The representation yields a monotone escort profile and a convex order potential. We use these to reformulate one-shot testing converses, exact sandwiched strong-converse exponents, and work-extraction reliability as signed-area or level-crossing statements, and discuss a restricted two-parameter pair-conversion rate.

</details>

### [Laziness of Quantum Walks on Graphs](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20739v1)

**Authors:** Amulya Mohan, Christino Tamon, Yichi Xu, Hanmeng Zhan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.20739v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops tools to quantify the 'laziness' of quantum walks on graphs by analyzing the trace of the average mixing matrix. It proves that graph structure dictates this invariant, establishing that the complete graph is the laziest connected graph and that the star graph is the laziest tree. The work is significant for understanding how graph topology governs long-term quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'laziness' provides a novel graph invariant derived from quantum dynamics, linking spectral properties (Laplacian eigenvalues) directly to physical return probabilities, which is relevant for understanding quantum transport in structured media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the 'laziness' of a graph, defined as the trace of the average mixing matrix of a quantum walk, which quantifies the long-term probability of a quantum walker returning to its starting point.

**Main result.** It establishes a hierarchy of laziness for graphs, showing that the complete graph $K_n$ is the laziest connected graph, and the star graph $S_n$ is the laziest tree on $n$ vertices.

**Method.** The analysis uses spectral graph theory, specifically examining the average mixing matrix $\mathcal{M}$ derived from the Laplacian quantum walk, and applying various algebraic tools like Schur products and spectral decomposition.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum walks evolving on an arbitrary graph $X$, governed by the Laplacian matrix $L$. The key diagnostic is the average mixing matrix $\mathcal{M} = \lim_{T	o\infty} rac{1}{T} \sum_{t=0}^{T} U(t) \circ U(t)$.

**Key observables.** Laziness, defined as $	ext{tr}(\mathcal{M})$, and the average mixing matrix $\mathcal{M}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of vertices $n$, and the specific structure of the graph (e.g., $K_n$, $S_n$, double stars $	ext{DS}(\ell, r)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of the Cesaro limit for the average mixing matrix and relies heavily on spectral properties of the Laplacian matrix.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops tools (properties of laziness) to prove the hierarchy of laziness for specific graph families, comparing structures like $K_n$, $S_n$, and various multipartite graphs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The trace of the average mixing matrix of a quantum walk measures the "laziness" of the walk: the higher the trace, the more likely that the walker returns home in the long run. In this paper, we develop tools to study this graph invariant arising from Laplacian quantum walks.   It is known that the complete graph $K_n$ is the laziest connected graph on $n$ vertices. Using our machinery, we show that the star $S_n$ is the second laziest connected graph on $n$ vertices (and hence the laziest tree on $n$ vertices), the complete multipartite graph $K_{n-2,1,1}$ is the third laziest connected graph on $n$ vertices, and the double star $DS(n-3,1)$ is the second laziest tree on $n$ vertices. We also show that on the same number of vertices, more unbalanced double stars are lazier.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper conducts a large-scale topological analysis of metabolic networks from diverse life forms. It introduces a refined method for building these networks based on direct chemical transformations and uses advanced community detection to quantify their structure. The findings reveal that the organization of these biological networks possesses specific topological signatures that cannot be explained by random chance, pointing to underlying evolutionary mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject is biochemistry, the rigorous application of graph theory, community detection (Surprise function), and statistical comparison against null models mirrors techniques used in analyzing complex systems in statistical physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To comprehensively analyze the topological properties and community structure of metabolic networks across diverse life domains, identifying features driven by evolutionary pressures beyond simple degree distribution.

**Main result.** Metabolic networks exhibit distinct topological signatures, such as being disassortative and having a higher local clustering coefficient compared to random models, suggesting underlying evolutionary constraints.

**Method.** The study employs novel network construction methods focusing on direct chemical transformations and uses advanced community detection algorithms like Surprise to compare real networks against randomized null models.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a metabolic network, modeled as a graph where nodes are metabolites and edges represent direct chemical transformations. The analysis spans 10912 organisms across Bacteria, Archaea, and Eukarya.

**Key observables.** Community structure metrics (Surprise function, $N_c$, $ar{n}_c$, $PI$), topological properties ($ar{k}$, $ar{C}$, $C$, $A$, $ar{d}$), and comparisons against randomized network metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis compares real vs. randomized networks using statistical tests (e.g., t-test) and focuses on identifying structural deviations from random expectations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that topological features not explained by degree distribution reflect genuine, non-random evolutionary pressures. The method also assumes the ability to resolve currency metabolites based on elemental balance.

**Figures summary.** The paper compares traditional vs. novel network constructions (Fig 1), presents statistical summaries of topological properties (Table 2), details community structure analysis (Table 5), and compares these findings against randomized network models (Tables 6 and 7).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the problem and novel network construction (focusing on chemical pathways) to detailing the statistical analysis of global and community metrics, culminating in a rigorous comparison between real biological networks and randomized null models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Metabolic networks play pivotal roles in understanding the evolution of organisms, microbiome dynamics and disease prevention and treatment. This study presents a comprehensive analysis of metabolic network properties across 10912 organisms spanning Bacteria, Archaea, and Eukarya domains. A novel method for the network construction is introduced, emphasizing the chemical transformations of metabolites. Unlike conventional approaches that link every substrate to every product in all chemical reactions, this method aligns more closely with metabolic pathways, linking products only to their generating substrates. Emphasis is placed on investigating the community structure within metabolic networks, employing the Surprise quality function that better addresses some limitations of previous approaches. Through comparisons between real and randomized versions of the networks, we identify characteristics that cannot be explained solely by the network degree distribution, thus identifying topological properties and community structures that arise from additional evolutionary pressures. This highlights the need for evolutionary models to incorporate additional mechanisms beyond the replication of the degree distribution.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.20660v1>

**Summary.** This research tackles the challenge of efficiently simulating Continuous-Time Quantum Walks by comparing two ways to decompose the system's Hamiltonian. By training machine learning models on graph properties, the authors developed a predictor that can advise quantum compilers on which decomposition minimizes costly CX gates. This offers a practical tool for optimizing quantum circuit compilation for quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource optimization bottleneck in simulating quantum dynamics on near-term quantum hardware by providing a data-driven tool to select the best circuit decomposition.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to predict which of two Hamiltonian decompositions (Pauli vs. matching) will result in fewer CX gates when simulating a Continuous-Time Quantum Walk (CTQW) via Trotterization.

**Main result.** The choice of the resource-efficient decomposition is primarily governed by the decomposition property $n_{	ext{Pauli}}$, rather than intrinsic graph topology. The trained ML model shows strong transferability, achieving high performance on larger, unseen graphs.

**Method.** A supervised machine learning approach is used to classify the optimal decomposition by training models on a large set of graphs, using graph topology and decomposition term counts as features.

**Model / system.** The system is a CTQW evolving on a graph $G$, whose time evolution $e^{-iHt}$ must be approximated using Trotterization. The comparison is between the Pauli and matching decompositions of the Hamiltonian $H$.

**Key observables.** The number of CX (two-qubit) gates required by each decomposition, and the classification accuracy (MCC) of the prediction model.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of vertices ($N$), the graph structure (e.g., 8-vertex graphs, Erdos-Renyi), and the feature counts ($n_{	ext{Pauli}}$, $n_{	ext{match}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation relies on Trotterization, and the selection criterion is solely based on minimizing the CX gate count, ignoring circuit depth or connectivity.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention tables comparing performance metrics (precision, recall, MCC) across various ML models, feature subsets, and increasing graph sizes ($N=8$ to $N=256$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem (Hamiltonian decomposition for CTQW), develops the methodology (feature engineering using graph and decomposition properties), trains ML models on a known population, and validates the model's predictive power on larger, held-out test sets.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating a continuous-time quantum walk (CTQW) on a graph in the circuit model of quantum computing requires decomposing its Hamiltonian into terms that can be Trotterized into hardware-native gates. We consider two such decompositions: the standard Pauli decomposition and the recently introduced matching decomposition. Prior work suggests that the matching decomposition uses fewer CX gates on sparse graphs, while the Pauli decomposition uses fewer on denser graphs. Since CX gates dominate error and runtime on current hardware, we train machine learning models to predict, for a given graph, which of the two decompositions produces the smaller CX gate count. We train and evaluate on the complete population of all 11,117 connected eight-vertex graphs from Brendan McKay's database, so the class balance and overlap are measured directly rather than estimated. We use twelve features: ten topological properties of the graph and two that count the terms the Pauli and matching decompositions produce (n_Pauli and n_match), both computable without transpiling the simulation circuit. Standard topological properties alone provide little predictive power. Instead, the dominant signal comes from n_Pauli, a property of the Hamiltonian decomposition rather than an intrinsic property of the graph; degree variance is the only other feature that carries signal. Across a range of models the Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC) falls in a narrow band, from 0.569 untuned to 0.593 after tuning, so no single architecture stands out. We adopt a single-hidden-layer neural network at MCC 0.593. Applied frozen to a held-out, class-balanced test set of larger graphs (up to 256 vertices) from structured and Erdos-Renyi families, the model transfers, with MCC rising from 0.785 at N=8 to 1 at N>=64.

</details>

### [Quantifying Entangling Power of Controlled Unitary Gates](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21105v1)

**Authors:** Ankur Haldar, Pankaj Agrawal, Prasenjit Deb  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.21105v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

(42), evaluated via the Newton’s- identity recursion of Eq. (40), at truncation orders N = 5 and N = 10 (dashed). Entropy is reported in bits, so the physical range is S ∈[0, 1] over ζ ∈[0, 1/4].</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, computable quantity, $\zeta$, to quantify the entanglement generated by controlled unitary gates in quantum computation. It establishes theoretical upper bounds for this entangling power, showing that the controlled unitary architecture can achieve optimal entanglement for specific dimension pairings. This provides a powerful, state-independent tool for analyzing quantum circuit performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a simple, computable metric ($\zeta$) that bypasses full state simulation is highly valuable for analyzing complex quantum circuits, providing theoretical benchmarks for quantum hardware performance.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a computable quantity to quantify the entangling power generated by controlled unitary gates, avoiding the need to simulate the full output state.

**Main result.** A new computable quantity, $\zeta$, is introduced, which successfully quantifies entanglement and establishes a universal upper bound $\zeta_{	ext{max}} = (2d-1)/(2d+1)$ for the generalized controlled unitary architecture.

**Method.** The authors derive $\zeta$ using properties of reduced density matrices and trace overlaps, relating it to established measures like purity and linear entropy.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-qubit and multi-qubit systems governed by Controlled Unitary (CU) and Generalized Controlled Unitary (GCU) gates acting on bipartite control and target registers.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is $\zeta$, the computable entangling power. Other key diagnostics include purity ($\gamma$), normalized linear entropy ($\zeta_r$), and von Neumann entropy ($S(ho)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the control ($m$) and target ($n$) registers, and the probability distribution $\{p_i\}$ over the control basis states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ideal quantum operations and derives bounds based on specific controlled unitary architectures, noting limitations when extending to arbitrary dimensions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the entanglement ratio $\zeta_r$ for the Toffoli gate, showing regions of separability and maximal entanglement, and compare theoretical entropy calculations against series approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining $\zeta$ for two-qubit gates, then generalizing it to GCU gates, deriving the universal upper bound $\zeta_{	ext{max}}$, and finally establishing functional relations between $\zeta$ and other entropy measures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Applying controlled unitary gates to generate entanglement between qubits is a routine task in both quantum communication and computation. The existing tools for predicting how much entanglement a given gate can generate require either simulation of the entangling circuit or averaging over a distribution of inputs for a given controlled unitary gate. Here, we introduce a computable quantity $ζ$ that not only determines whether a controlled unitary gate generates entanglement, but also quantifies the entanglement for any specific input without requiring the construction of the output state. For two-qubit controlled unitary gates, we establish the physical conditions corresponding to the extremum values of the proposed quantity. Extending the dimension of control and target registers to arbitrary size through a generalized controlled unitary architecture, we derive a universal upper bound on $ζ$ and identify the conditions for its saturation. Later we establish functional relation between the quantity and other known quantities, such as purity, normalized linear entropy, and von Neumann entropy. Finally, we compare our results with previous research work and show that the quantity proposed in this work achieves the previously known optimal values of entangling power of controlled unitary gates for some specific dimensions of target and control registers.

</details>

**Authors:** Hiroshi Ohno  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.20603v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how the performance of parameterized quantum circuits scales by comparing several metrics of model size. The authors find that while simple counts like parameter number ($P$) or Lie algebra dimensions ($	ext{DLA}$, $	ext{OOD}$) are insufficient, the Jacobian effective dimension ($	ext{Deff}$) provides a robust, geometry-aware measure of the circuit's accessible degrees of freedom. This suggests $	ext{Deff}$ should be used instead of $P$ when characterizing model capacity.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, mathematically grounded metric ($	ext{Deff}$) to quantify the true complexity or capacity of variational quantum circuits, which is crucial for designing scalable quantum machine learning algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize how the performance (loss) of parameterized quantum circuits scales with available resources, such as data size, model size, and computational effort.

**Main result.** The Jacobian effective dimension ($	ext{Deff}$) was shown to be a geometry-aware measure that scales comparably to the nominal parameter size ($P$) and is superior to other Lie-algebraic measures ($	ext{DLA}$, $	ext{OOD}$) which rapidly saturate.

**Method.** The authors numerically investigated scaling laws by training the circuits on a linear regression task using Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) minimization via SPSA.

**Model / system.** The model is a parameterized quantum circuit (PQC) defined by a unitary $U(	heta) = \prod e^{-i	heta_j G_j}$, where generators $G_j$ are sampled from Pauli strings.

**Key observables.** Loss (RMSE), Dimension of dynamical Lie algebra ($	ext{DLA}$), Observable orbit dimension ($	ext{OOD}$), and Jacobian effective dimension ($	ext{Deff}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Training sample size ($M$), parameter size ($P$), and number of optimization iterations ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to a regression task and small qubit numbers (four and eight), and the scaling laws are observed over finite ranges.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decrease in loss with increasing $M$, $P$, and $T$. Figure 3 compares $	ext{DLA}$, $	ext{OOD}$, and $	ext{Deff}$ vs. $P$, showing saturation for the first two and strong correlation for $	ext{Deff}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need to measure resource scaling, analyzes data/model/compute scaling using various metrics ($	ext{DLA}$, $	ext{OOD}$, $	ext{Deff}$), presents results showing $	ext{Deff}$'s superior scaling behavior, and concludes by proposing $	ext{Deff}$ as a robust geometric measure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how the performance of parameterized quantum circuits scales with available resources is important for characterizing their trainability and effective model capacity. In this study, we numerically investigate data scaling, model scaling, and compute scaling in parameterized quantum circuits and examine Lie-algebraic quantities as alternative measures of model size. In addition to the number of circuit parameters, we consider the dimension of the dynamical Lie algebra, the observable-orbit dimension, and a Jacobian effective dimension defined as the rank of the Jacobian of the parameterized observable orbit. Using a regression task with randomly generated Pauli-string generators, we observe decreasing loss with increasing training dataset size, parameter size, and number of optimization iterations over the ranges investigated. For model scaling, the dynamical Lie algebra and observable orbit dimensions rapidly saturate as the parameter size increases, whereas the Jacobian effective dimension remains strongly correlated with the parameter size and exhibits comparable scaling behavior. These results suggest that the Jacobian effective dimension provides a geometry-aware measure of the locally accessible observable degrees of freedom of finite-depth parameterized quantum circuits and may serve as a useful scaling parameter beyond the nominal parameter size.

</details>

### [Tools for Reducing Service Time in Near-Term Quantum Networks](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20954v1)

**Authors:** Jake Smith, Thomas R. Beauchamp, Scarlett Gauthier, Oumayma Bouchmal, Stephanie Wehner  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.20954v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

At α = 0 (top) the full minimum separation is kept and the network idles after an early tgen. At α &gt; 0 (bottom) the separation shortens by αE, reclaiming idle time. A packet generated within the shaded ingress region risks a minimum separation violation (Section IV-B).</sub>

Both quantities are normalized by the window length w to enable comparison across demands with different PGA durations. Each grid point is estimated from 104 successful trials obtained by rejection sampling. White contours are spaced at intervals of 0.1. These surfaces are computed once per physical configuration (λ, w, s) and stored as lookup tables for use by the Markov chain session model.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel method to optimize resource utilization in near-term quantum networks by intelligently shortening the mandatory idle time scheduled between entanglement generation attempts. By analytically modeling the scheduling process, the authors demonstrate significant reductions in total service time, especially when multiple applications share the quantum hardware. This is crucial for making quantum network services more efficient and practical.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-management optimization technique applicable to the scheduling overhead inherent in building and operating quantum communication infrastructure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the inefficiency in near-term quantum networks caused by mandatory idle time scheduled between entanglement generation attempts.

**Main result.** The proposed optimization method can reduce the total network service time by up to 30% when multiple applications are co-scheduled.

**Method.** It uses an analytical execution model, treating the scheduling problem as an optimization to shorten the scheduled separation time while maintaining required service probabilities.

**Model / system.** The system is a multi-user quantum network utilizing the Arqon architecture, which generates shared entanglement for quantum information applications.

**Key observables.** Network service time reduction (up to 30% for co-scheduled apps), session execution speedup, and the optimized scheduled separation time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Minimum separation time ($t_{minsep}$), service probability, and the degree of co-scheduling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimization assumes a balance between minimizing time and ensuring the target number of instances is completed with a specified service probability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the comparison between full minimum separation and reduced separation in the scheduling process.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the scheduling limitation, proposes the analytical optimization method, applies it to the Arqon architecture, and presents quantitative results comparing reduced vs. baseline service times.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Architectures have been proposed to control entanglement generation in multi-user quantum networks. To allow time for local operations and classical communication at end nodes, these architectures insert fixed separations between consecutive batches of entanglement generation attempts. This reduces network utilization when attempts fail, leaving the network idle during the scheduled separation. To address this limitation, we propose a novel method to reclaim this idle time by shortening the scheduled separation between attempts while respecting hardware constraints. The method uses an analytical execution model to optimize the separation and reduce the total network service time of an application. Evaluations within the Arqon architecture show network service time reductions of up to 42 minutes (7.6%) for single applications and 16-29 minutes (26-30%) per application when co-scheduled. The approach applies broadly to quantum network architectures that share hardware between entanglement generation and local operations, and the method can be used online by network schedulers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates phase control over magnon-phonon coupling in magnetic thin films by varying an external magnetic field. By tuning the coupling constant's phase from real to complex, the system can couple to overdamped magnons. This mechanism is experimentally observable as a distinct minimum in the SAW transmission spectrum near zero magnetic field.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to tune the coupling phase via magnetic field provides a clean platform to explore non-Hermitian physics and dissipative coupling mechanisms in solid-state magnonics, relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the phase of the magnon-phonon coupling constant can be controlled by tuning an external magnetic field in magnetic thin films.

**Main result.** The phase control allows for coupling to overdamped magnons via an imaginary coupling component ($\Phi = -\pi/2$), which manifests as a pronounced minimum in the SAW transmittance spectrum near zero magnetic field.

**Method.** The research combines theoretical modeling using coupled equations of motion (LLG and elastic wave equations) with analysis of measurable quantities like SAW transmittance.

**Model / system.** The system is a magnetic thin film, specifically a Ni/Ru/Ni synthetic antiferromagnet, where magnons interact with surface acoustic waves (SAW) phonons.

**Key observables.** SAW transmittance ($\lvert S_{21} vert^2$), the phase ($\Phi$) of the coupling constant, and the magnon eigenfrequency ($\omega_m$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic field ($\mu_0H$), magnetic damping ($\alpha$), and the transition field ($H_{tp}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analyses neglect magnetic anisotropy and magnetic dipole interactions, though later work incorporates these effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the dependence of SAW transmittance on magnetic field, illustrating dips associated with both $\Phi=0$ and $\Phi=-\pi/2$ coupling regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the coupled equations of motion, analyzes the complex nature of the coupling constant, and then calculates the resulting SAW transmittance spectrum under varying magnetic field conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the phase of the coupling between magnons and surface acoustic wave (SAW) phonons in magnetic thin films. The coupling constant changes from a real to a complex number as the external magnetic field is reduced. Below a transition field, the imaginary coupling constant allows SAW phonons to couple to overdamped magnons whose resonance frequency is close to zero and far from the SAW resonance. The strength of the imaginary coupling constant and the magnitude of the transition field both scale with magnetic damping. We find the coupling produces a broad, pronounced minimum in the SAW transmittance spectrum near zero magnetic field in a Ni/Ru/Ni synthetic antiferromagnet with large magnetic damping. These results demonstrate that the phase of the complex magnon-phonon coupling constant can be tuned via magnetic field in strongly damped magnets, offering a platform to explore novel regimes of magnon-phonon interactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work details the controlled fabrication of highly-ordered plasmonic nanostructures on thin gold films using a double-pulse femtosecond laser approach. By managing the transient fluid dynamics through optimized pulse timing, the researchers suppress chaotic melting and achieve structures exhibiting narrow Surface Lattice Resonances. These highly ordered plasmonic arrays are presented as a robust platform for next-generation, ultrasensitive biosensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed coupling between electromagnetic excitation (plasmons) and complex non-equilibrium thermofluid dynamics (Marangoni flow) to achieve a desired quantum-optical feature (narrow resonance) is highly relevant to open quantum systems and light-matter interaction in structured media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to fabricate highly-ordered Laser-Induced Periodic Surface Structures (LIPSS) on thin metallic films for use in high-sensitivity plasmonic biosensing.

**Main result.** The use of a double-pulse femtosecond laser scheme, optimized with a specific interpulse delay, successfully controls the fluid dynamics, leading to highly-uniform LIPSS that exhibit narrow Surface Lattice Resonances (SLRs).

**Method.** The study combines experimental techniques (femtosecond laser processing, SEM, ellipsometry) with theoretical modeling (3D-FDTD, Two-Temperature Model, Navier-Stokes equations).

**Model / system.** The system involves thin gold films where the process is governed by a synergy of coupled Surface Plasmon Polaritons (SPPs) excitation, electron-lattice thermalization, and subsequent Marangoni fluid flow dynamics.

**Key observables.** Surface morphology (SEM), resonance spectral position ($\lambda_{	ext{Res}}$), and resonance linewidth (FWHM, Quality factor Q).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interpulse delay ($\Delta	au \approx 1.2$ ns), film thickness (32 nm), and laser fluence ($\Phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formation process is modeled as a three-step sequence: plasmonic energy deposition, electron thermalization, and fluid dynamics, with the single-pulse regime being dominated by uncontrolled hydrodynamic instabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare LIPSS patterns from single vs. double pulses, show lattice temperature profiles, and present ellipsometry data confirming the narrow SLR signatures at specific wavelengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a multiscale model encompassing electromagnetic field calculation (FDTD), thermal modeling (TTM), and fluid dynamics (NSE). It then demonstrates that double-pulse irradiation controls the melt dynamics, leading to the formation of highly ordered structures exhibiting measurable plasmonic resonances.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The fabrication of highly ordered laser-induced periodic surface structures (LIPSS) on thin metallic films is dictated, predomaninatly, by a synergy of periodic electromagnetic energy deposition and complex fluid dynamics. In this work, we present a combined experimental and theoretical study on the formation of ultra-regular LIPSS on 32-nm-thick Au films using a double-pulse femtosecond laser scheme. We demonstrate that for thin films, the excitation of coupled Surface Plasmon Polaritons (SPPs) at both interfaces dictates the initial energy distribution. On the othe hand, the final morphology is greatly influenced by hydrodynamical processes. Interestingly, due to the low electron-phonon coupling of Au and the high thermal confinement of the thin film, single-pulse irradiation leads to uncontrolled hydrodynamic instabilities a non uniform topographies. Thus, we demonstrate that a double-pulse approach with an optimized interpulse delay (Δτ=1.2 ns) effectively controls the melt duration and viscosity, suppressing complex fluid motion and promoting the growth of highly ordered arrays. These structures support narrow surface lattice resonances (SLRs) suitable for high-sensitivity plasmonic biosensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the experimental observation and theoretical modeling of spectral anisotropy in transition radiation emitted from biaxial van der Waals crystals. By rotating the sample and measuring polarization-resolved spectra, the authors prove that TR is highly sensitive to the material's orientation-dependent dielectric response. This opens up optical anisotropy as a new diagnostic tool for free electron-driven spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is in condensed matter optics, the technique itself—using electron-beam induced radiation to map anisotropic dielectric tensors—is a powerful, non-destructive diagnostic tool applicable to characterizing complex quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the spectral anisotropy of transition radiation (TR) is influenced by the orientation-dependent dielectric response of biaxial optical media.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrate and theoretically model that TR spectra exhibit pronounced, measurable anisotropy when measured from biaxial crystals, confirming that TR is a sensitive probe of the material's optical axis dependence.

**Method.** The research combines experimental cathodoluminescence (CL) spectroscopy, involving sample rotation and polarization-selective detection, with the development of a thin-film theoretical model.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves biaxial van der Waals crystals (like GeS and MoOCl2) acting as the source. The model adapts the standard TR intensity formula to incorporate the two principal in-plane dielectric responses ($\varepsilon_{xx}$ and $\varepsilon_{yy}$) to predict orientation-dependent spectra.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the polarization-resolved transition radiation spectra, measured as a function of the crystal's rotation angle ($\phi$) and the electron beam energy ($E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the crystal's principal dielectric axes, the electron beam energy (10–30 keV), and the orientation angle ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical model uses an effective projection model to combine principal-axis intensities, which is noted as an approximation compared to a full vectorial treatment of the dielectric tensor.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the polarization-selective CL geometry, show angle-resolved spectra for isotropic materials (WS2), and demonstrate the periodic spectral changes and energy tunability for anisotropic materials (MoOCl2) along their principal axes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem of anisotropy in TR, details the experimental setup using CL spectroscopy on biaxial crystals, develops an anisotropic thin-film model, and presents results showing the successful reproduction of angle-dependent spectra and energy shifts.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In anisotropic optical media, the electromagnetic response depends on the orientation of the optical field relative to the material's principal dielectric axes. While this direction dependence is well understood in conventional optics, it should also influence light-generation processes driven by free electrons. Here, we experimentally observe spectrally anisotropic transition radiation from biaxial van der Waals crystals. Using cathodoluminescence spectroscopy on germanium sulphide (GeS) and molybdenum oxydichloride (MoOCl$_2$) crystals, we show that the transition-radiation spectra differ along the principal in-plane optical axes. To describe this spectral anisotropy, we develop a thin-film transition-radiation model that reproduces the experimental observations. Our results demonstrate that transition radiation is a sensitive probe of the axis-dependent dielectric response of biaxial optical media and suggest that optical anisotropy can provide an additional degree of freedom for free electron-driven spectroscopy, radiation sources, and transition-radiation-based diagnostics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that emergent geometric symmetries dictate the formation of Weyl nodes in interacting multi-terminal Josephson junctions. By confining these topological singularities to a specific manifold ($\chi$-manifold), the authors provide a robust, gate-tunable design principle for creating quantum devices exhibiting non-trivial topological properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between geometric symmetries, topological invariants (Weyl nodes), and tunable superconducting junctions provides a powerful, symmetry-protected design principle for realizing exotic quantum states in solid-state devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the mechanism by which an emergent geometric symmetry generates non-trivial band topology, specifically Weyl nodes, within interacting multi-terminal Josephson junctions (MTJJs).

**Main result.** The symmetry confines the topological singularities (Weyl nodes) to an analytically defined manifold ($\chi$-manifold), providing a general design principle for fabricating devices with predictable topological signatures.

**Method.** The analysis combines analytical derivations in the Atomic Limit (AL) approximation with numerical simulations using Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) on the full interacting model.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum-dot-based multi-terminal Josephson Junction (MTJJ) modeled by a Hamiltonian involving dot energies, interdot couplings, and superconducting self-energies. The physics is analyzed by examining spectral symmetries.

**Key observables.** Weyl nodes (with charges $\pm 1$ in the singlet sector and $\pm 2$ in the doublet sector), Chern numbers, and the gap opening between same-spin Andreev bound states (ABS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interdot coupling ($t_{LR}$), on-site repulsion ($U$), interaction asymmetry ($d_U$), and the geometric symmetry parameters ($\chi_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis heavily relies on the Atomic Limit (AL) approximation for deriving analytic boundary conditions, although these are compared against full DMRG results.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate analytic phase diagrams showing the predicted locations of Weyl nodes on the $\chi$-manifold, and subsequent figures show how these nodes are affected (e.g., gap opening) when symmetries are explicitly broken by interaction asymmetries.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the Hamiltonian and the role of geometric symmetries ($P_\alpha$), derives the analytic conditions for topological crossings on the $\chi$-manifold in the AL, and then validates these predictions by analyzing the full interacting model using DMRG sweeps.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that an emergent geometric symmetry generates non-trivial topology in quantum-dot-based multi-terminal Josephson junctions. It confines same-spin Andreev bound state crossings to an analytic one-dimensional manifold of the synthetic Brillouin zone, where interdot coupling selects Weyl nodes of charge $\pm1$ in the singlet sector and doubly degenerate cones of charge $\pm2$ in the doublet sector, at gate-tunable locations. The mechanism yields a spectroscopic detection protocol and a design principle for fabricating devices with non-trivial topological signatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates Artificial Spin Ices on complex, aperiodic lattices, moving beyond simple periodic structures. Using Monte Carlo simulations, the authors map out the low-energy magnetic configurations and identify how the lattice geometry dictates the formation of magnetic domains and topological defects like monopoles. This work advances the understanding of frustrated magnetism in engineered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study uses classical statistical mechanics tools (MC, entropy calculation) to model complex magnetic frustration, which shares conceptual links with frustrated quantum spin systems and domain dynamics in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study extends Artificial Spin Ice (ASI) simulations from periodic tilings to aperiodic hexagonal tilings to understand how lattice geometry affects magnetic ground states.

**Main result.** The aperiodic tiling introduces new vertex types and demonstrates that the system's low-energy states are characterized by domain boundaries and localized magnetic charges (monopoles).

**Method.** The research employs Metropolis Monte Carlo simulations to cool the spin arrays to low-energy configurations, supplemented by micromagnetic simulations for vertex energy analysis.

**Model / system.** The system consists of artificial spin ices (ASIs) modeled as arrays of nanomagnets on geometric lattices (e.g., single edge length tilings). The magnetic interactions are governed by a Hamiltonian including nearest-neighbor and dipolar terms.

**Key observables.** Low-energy spin configurations, residual entropy ($S_r$), vertex populations (E0, E1, E2), and the formation/location of magnetic charges (monopoles).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling ratio $|J/D| = 1.95$; simulation size of $\approx 3114$ islands; comparison between periodic (dice) and aperiodic tilings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Open boundary conditions are assumed to be sufficient, and the analysis focuses on the classical energy landscape of the spin system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between periodic and aperiodic tilings, show specific heat and energy per spin for different geometries, and map out the final spin states revealing domain boundaries and monopole charges.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model on simple tilings, then extending the analysis to aperiodic structures, and finally detailing the physics of domain wall formation and monopole behavior when multiple ground states are present.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial spin ices (ASIs) are decorations of single domain nanomagnets on geometric lattices allowing for study of magnetic interactions on easily varied lattice geometries. We extend the study of ASIs on single rhomb tilings by simulating an ASI with the aperiodic hexagonal $H_{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{2}}$ tiling as the base structure, and the periodic dice lattice for additional comparison. Using a point dipole metropolis Monte Carlo approach, we cool our arrays of nanoislands to form a low energy configuration, assessing individual vertex excitations as identified through complementary micromagnetic simulations. Using the energy and charge landscape of final spin states, we propose a potential ground state spin configuration for the aperiodic system.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel design for achieving continuously tunable ferroelectric polarization by exploiting superlubric sliding in incommensurate 2D material heterojunctions. Using DFT calculations, the authors predict ultra-low switching barriers ($\mu	ext{eV}$) driven by a low vertical electric field. This breakthrough combines the best features of superlubricity and ferroelectricity for advanced electronic applications like artificial synapses.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the concept of controlling polarization via low-energy, electrically driven sliding interfaces presents a novel mechanism for energy-efficient switching, which has parallels in designing quantum memory or switching elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to combine the low-energy switching of sliding ferroelectricity with the ultra-low friction of superlubric sliding to achieve continuous polarization control.

**Main result.** The authors propose a design where vertical polarization can be continuously controlled via superlubric sliding of incommensurate interfaces, achieving switching barriers as low as the $\mu	ext{eV}$ magnitude.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling using Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, specifically employing the Nudged Elastic Band (NEB) method to map out sliding pathways and calculate polarization.

**Model / system.** The system involves lateral heterojunctions formed by stacking 2D materials (e.g., graphene/BN or Gr/TMDs) in an incommensurate configuration, allowing for superlubric sliding.

**Key observables.** Continuous tunable vertical polarization ($P$), sliding switching barrier ($\mu	ext{eV}$ magnitude), and long ion displacements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low vertical voltage driving force, lattice mismatch leading to incommensurate interfaces, and thermal stability relative to $k_B T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanism relies on the interface becoming incommensurate upon large twist angles, and the total sliding barrier must exceed $k_B T$ for stability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the superlubric device design based on bilayer lateral junctions, showing polarization switching under an electric field, and detail the calculated sliding pathway barriers.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the challenge of combining superlubricity and switchable ferroelectricity. It then proposes the heterojunction design, details the DFT/NEB calculations to predict polarization control and low barriers, and concludes by discussing the implications for artificial synaptic devices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sliding ferroelectricity widely exists in various van der Waals bilayers or multilayers, which is induced by asymmetric stacking of commensurate interface. The greatly reduced switching barriers via interlayer sliding lead to high speed with low energy cost, while they are still much higher compared with superlubric sliding of incommensurate interfaces. The polarizations of such incommensurate interfaces are not switchable, which is the major obstacle of combing superlubricity and sliding ferroelectricity for ultralow barriers. Here we propose a design of such combination based on previous synthesis of lateral heterojunctions of 2D materials, which can be extensively applicable to various systems including PN junctions. In such long-distance superlubric ferroelectricity, the vertical polarization can be continuously controlled by superlubric sliding of incommensurate interfaces between lateral heterojunction bilayers, where the series of multiple stable states are long-sought for artificial synaptic devices. The unconventionality of our findings does not only include unprecedented barriers down to the magnitude of mu-eV, but also unprecedented long ion displacements distinct from the small deviations in classical paradigm of ferroelectricity. Our predicted superlubric sliding electrically driven by low vertical voltage is also hitherto reported, much more efficient compared with previously reported sliding mechanically driven by tips, resolving a major issue for practical applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically proposes planar nanofluidic memristors that utilize ion transport in nanochannels to achieve memory effects. By employing the Poisson-Nernst-Planck framework, the authors show that a surface charge gradient is key to generating hysteresis. The results provide a quantitative design method based on controlling the spatial pattern of surface charge.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state devices, the underlying physics—non-equilibrium ion transport, diffusion-mediated memory effects, and the use of continuum equations (PNP)—shares mathematical parallels with modeling charge dynamics in quantum systems or complex electrochemical interfaces.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the theoretical design and optimization of planar nanofluidic memristors by exploiting ion transport mechanisms.

**Main result.** Memristive behavior can be achieved using a surface charge gradient, and the memory effect is shown to be governed by the first-order moment of the surface charge, providing a design rule.

**Method.** The study employs the Poisson-Nernst-Planck (PNP) framework, solving the coupled partial differential equations numerically to analyze ion dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a planar nanochannel where ion transport dictates the memristive switching behavior. The model uses the PNP equations to describe ionic fluxes, potential, and concentration changes.

**Key observables.** Hysteretic I-V loops, memory area (A), characteristic memory time (T_op), and the first-order moment of the surface charge (M_chi).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Channel height ($\gamma$), relative length ($	heta$), surface charge density ($\chi$), and the ratio of diffusion time to drift time.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis neglects fluidic convection (electro-osmosis) and assumes ideal boundary conditions like strong reservoirs and blocking walls.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simulated I-V curves showing hysteresis onset with surface charge gradients, and plots demonstrate the dependence of memory area and characteristic time on various physical parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the PNP model, analyzes the conditions for memristive switching (requiring a surface charge gradient), derives the relationship between memory effect and the first moment of surface charge, and finally provides scaling laws for the characteristic memory time.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanofluidic memristors, exploiting ion transport in nanochannels, hold promise for neuromorphic applications. A planar architecture is particularly desired for scalable integration with established micro- and nanofabrication technologies. Here, using the Poisson-Nernst-Planck framework, we theoretically propose planar nanofluidic memristors enabled by surface charge gradient, providing an alternative to the commonly used geometrically asymmetric architectures. The resulting memristive behavior is governed by a diffusion-mediated secondary enrichment effect. By systematically solving the PNP equations, we obtain the scaling of the characteristic memory time across the parameter space. We also reveal that the memory effect is related to the first-order moment of surface charge, for arbitrary charge profiles. These results provide a theoretical basis for rationally designing and optimizing planar nanofluidic memristors through spatially patterned surface charge.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the stability and dynamics of localized wave packets governed by a saturable nonlinear Schrödinger equation with time- and space-dependent coefficients. Using variational methods and numerical simulations, the authors demonstrate that the stability of these structures is critically dependent on the modulation parameters. The findings offer crucial insights for controlling nonlinear wave phenomena in physical media.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as the NLS equation is a canonical model for describing nonlinear wave propagation in systems like optical waveguides and Bose-Einstein condensates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the dynamics and stability of localized solutions within a saturable nonlinear Schrödinger equation featuring space- and time-dependent variable coefficients.

**Main result.** The stability of the localized solutions is found to be highly sensitive to the modulation parameters, leading to the emergence of alternating stable and unstable regions.

**Method.** The research employs a combination of variational approaches, similarity transformations to achieve autonomous forms, and linear stability analysis via an eigenvalue problem, supplemented by direct numerical simulations.

**Model / system.** The core model is the saturable nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) with the general form $i\psi_t = -rac{1}{2}\psi_{xx} + V(x, t)\psi + g(x, t)|\psi|^2\psi + \gamma(x, t)|\psi|^2$. The analysis explores various non-autonomous potentials, including static, seesaw, flying-bird, and mixed potentials.

**Key observables.** Stability (determined by the sign of the eigenvalue $\lambda$), maximum amplitude ($h_{	ext{max}}$), and mean width ($\langle x^2 angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation parameters (e.g., modulation frequency $\omega_0$, and parameters $\sigma$ used in potentials), and variational parameters $A$ and $B$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The variational method relies on a Taylor series expansion for the logarithmic term, and the stability analysis for the autonomous case is separate from the full non-autonomous system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the real part of the eigenvalue $\lambda$ vs. energy $E$ for the autonomous case, and show the evolution of $h_{	ext{max}}$ and $\langle x^2 angle$ for various modulation potentials (static, seesaw, flying-bird, mixed) as a function of modulation frequency $\omega_0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general NLS model and applying variational/similarity techniques to derive an autonomous form. It then analyzes stability using eigenvalue problems and concludes with direct numerical simulations testing various time-dependent potentials to map out stability regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we study the dynamics and stability of localized solutions in a saturable nonlinear Schrödinger equation with space- and time-dependent variable coefficients. Using a variational approach and numerical simulations, we analyze the effects of different external potential configurations. Our results reveal that the stability of the solutions is highly sensitive to the modulation parameters, leading to the emergence of alternating stable and unstable regions as a function of the modulation frequency. These findings provide valuable insights into the control of localized structures in nonlinear wave systems, with potential implications for optical waveguides, Bose-Einstein condensates, and other nonlinear media.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work models how opposite helical edge states in QSH insulators couple when interfaced with superconductors. By analyzing transport signatures like zero-bias conductance and superconducting gap structures, the authors map out the microscopic parameters—such as barrier geometry and mismatch—that control this interedge coupling. This provides crucial insight into the functionality of topological superconducting hybrid devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed control over superconducting signatures (gaps, interference patterns) via microscopic edge coupling mechanisms provides a rich platform for studying proximity effects and superconducting quantum interference in topological materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the microscopic conditions governing the coupling between opposite helical edge states in Quantum Spin Hall (QSH) insulators when they form hybrid junctions with superconductors (NS and SNS).

**Main result.** Interedge coupling causes measurable deviations from quantized conductance values in NS junctions and opens characteristic gaps at $\varphi=0$ and $\varphi=\pi$ in the Andreev bound state spectrum of SNS junctions.

**Method.** The study employs the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism combined with a tight-binding approach to model transport properties and superconducting gap structures.

**Model / system.** The system models QSH edge states coupled to superconducting leads, analyzed using a microscopic Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang model. The analysis covers both zero-bias conductance measurements (NS) and the superconducting phase dependence of Andreev bound states (SNS).

**Key observables.** Zero-bias Andreev conductance ($G$), Andreev Bound State (ABS) energies ($E$ vs. $\varphi$), and the Superconducting Quantum Interference (SQI) pattern.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Barrier gap, interface transparency, orbital/Fermi-velocity mismatch, disorder strength ($\lambda$), and superconducting phase difference ($\varphi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Simplifications include neglecting spin-nonconserving terms (like Rashba SOC) and using effective scatterer models for transport calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic junctions, conductance vs. width/bias plots, and energy spectra showing gap openings dependent on the superconducting phase difference $\varphi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the physics in NS junctions (conductance deviations) and then extends the analysis to SNS junctions (gap opening and SQI modification), incorporating effects of disorder and geometric parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the microscopic conditions that allow for the coupling between opposite quantum spin Hall (QSH) edges in hybrid junctions with superconductors. Using a microscopic Bernevig--Hughes--Zhang model and the Bogoliubov--de Gennes formalism, we model a potential barrier along the NS interface and identify the parameter regimes in which the QSH edges are coupled. In normal--superconductor junctions, such coupling manifests as deviations from the quantized zero-bias Andreev conductance $G=4e^2/h$. These deviations are controlled by the induced gap in the barrier, the barrier geometry, the interface transparency, orbital and Fermi-velocity mismatch, and disorder strength as well as the bias voltage leading to a zero-bias peak. We then analyze the impact of this interedge-coupling mechanism in Josephson junctions at equilibrium and show that it hybridizes the edge-resolved Andreev branches, opens gaps at the time-reversal-invariant phase differences $\varphi=0$ and $\varphi=π$, and modifies the superconducting quantum interference pattern. In a reflection-symmetric geometry, the relative sizes of the two gap openings provide complementary information about the interedge dynamical phase, which also determines the parity of the suppressed lobes in the magnetic interference pattern. This investigation sheds light on the microscopic details that control the coupling of helical edge states in actual devices and the resulting consequences for superconducting hybrid systems.

</details>

The dashed curves are the exact critical level lines at ε = εth. In the central region they go around the...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a microscopic theory to predict the existence and stability of Laughlin pairs—repulsively bound electron pairs—within quantum Hall edge channels. By analyzing the two-electron Hamiltonian using advanced techniques like complex scaling, the authors show that these pairs can be stabilized by the geometry of the confining potential. The results suggest that such correlated, anyonic excitations are potentially observable using current single-electron circuit technology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from strongly correlated electron physics (Laughlin states) with mesoscopic quantum transport, providing concrete, calculable predictions for observing exotic correlated states in solid-state devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the possibility of electron-electron repulsion driving quantized relative circulation, enabling two electrons to propagate together as a positive-energy Laughlin pair in a depleted quantum Hall edge channel.

**Main result.** The theory predicts that existing GaAs devices may already support the lowest spin-polarized Laughlin pair with a lifetime significantly longer than typical propagation times, suggesting experimental feasibility.

**Method.** The authors developed a microscopic theory using the physical two-electron Hamiltonian, employing complex scaling, analytic tunneling theory, and simulating dynamics with the full finite-field Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is a depleted quantum Hall edge channel where two interacting electrons are confined. The analysis uses the physical two-electron Hamiltonian projected onto the Lowest Landau Level (LLL) to model the correlated dynamics.

**Key observables.** Dissociation threshold, decay rates, quasibound resonance energies ($\epsilon_m - i\gamma_m$), and the pair's lifetime ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The stability depends on the electric-field gradient tensor ($\hat{V}$) and the ratio of Coulomb energy to characteristic energy scales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis involves local approximations of the potential (up to quadratic order) and assumes the collision pathway survives full two-dimensional dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows how the external electric-field gradient leads to quasibound resonances whose lifetimes depend on the distance from the dissociation threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and the theoretical framework for quasibound pairs to developing quantitative tools (complex scaling, tunneling theory) and concluding with simulations demonstrating pair creation and propagation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

On-demand sources and mesoscopic beam splitters allow individual ballistic electrons to collide in depleted quantum Hall edge channels, where their unscreened Coulomb interaction acts as a strong, controllable nonlinearity. Theory suggests a more striking possibility: in a strong magnetic field, the repulsion can drive quantized relative circulation, allowing two electrons to propagate together as a positive-energy Laughlin pair. The relevance of such pairs to experiment depends on quantitative lifetimes in realistic guiding potentials and on whether the proposed collision pathway to pair formation survives full two-dimensional dynamics. We develop a microscopic theory of quasibound Laughlin pairs using the physical two-electron Hamiltonian. For a general local electric-field gradient, we determine the dissociation threshold, number of quasibound states, and decay rates. Complex scaling and analytic tunneling theory show that lifetimes grow exponentially with pair energy above threshold. Applied to reported GaAs parameters, the theory indicates that existing devices may already support the lowest spin-polarized pair, with a leading lifetime estimate about three orders of magnitude longer than typical propagation times. We simulate a collision with the full finite-field Hamiltonian, demonstrating both a framework for nonlinear two-electron quantum dynamics and the creation of a Laughlin pair in a representative two-electron collision. We use Husimi distributions and their zeros to visualize both quasibound resonances and transient collision states in phase space. These results place the preparation, propagation, and detection of repulsively paired electrons within reach of existing single-electron circuit technology. They identify kinematic stabilization under constrained one-dimensional propagation as a pairing mechanism that may extend to anyonic quantum Hall edge excitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reviews the state-of-the-art in using Artificial Intelligence to advance polymer science, focusing heavily on the crucial role of molecular encoding strategies. It compares fingerprint, graph, and sequence-based representations for tasks like property prediction and inverse design. The work stresses that standardized, comprehensive databases and better encoding are necessary to move polymer discovery from empirical to AI-driven innovation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on polymers, the core methodology—applying advanced ML/AI (GNNs, Transformers, LLMs) to complex, high-dimensional structural data—is highly relevant to modeling quantum many-body systems or disordered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The field of AI-driven polymer science is lagging due to the structural complexity of polymers, requiring better encoding strategies for AI models.

**Main result.** The paper provides a critical review and practical guidance on leveraging advanced AI techniques for polymer design, emphasizing the need for standardized, multi-scale data.

**Method.** The authors critically examine various AI encoding strategies (Fingerprint, Graph, Sequence) and model architectures (GNNs, Transformers, LLMs) applied to polymer property prediction and inverse design.

**Model / system.** The physical system is polymers (homopolymers, copolymers, blends). The models involve deep learning architectures like Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Transformers, operating on molecular representations.

**Key observables.** Polymer properties such as glass transition temperature ($T_g$), mechanical strength, and electronic/dielectric properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Polymerization degree, structural connectivity (2D/3D graphs), and chemical composition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The choice of encoding must align with data availability and the structural complexity of the polymer system.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares polymer databases based on data coverage and property inclusion; Table 1 summarizes common encoding strategies (Fingerprint, Graph, Sequence) for various tasks.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the gap in AI application to polymers, then detailing the evolution and comparison of encoding strategies, evaluating model applicability in property prediction and inverse design, and concluding with recommendations for data infrastructure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial intelligence (AI) is redefining the landscape of polymer science. Although numerous AI applications have been introduced in this field, the roles of polymer encoding strategies and different applications of AI models in polymer design remain insufficiently understood. Here, we build upon the foundation of polymer databases to critically examine the performance and applicability of current encoding strategies across different use cases. We then focus on two major AI application domains, property prediction and inverse design, to evaluate the strengths, weaknesses and suitable scenarios for various model architectures. Finally, we emphasize the significance of the online platforms for promoting data accessibility and accelerating the migration from experience-based discovery toward AI-driven innovation in polymer science and engineering. Through these discussions, we aim to provide practical guidance for future research and development in AI-assisted polymer design.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines interband plasmons in 2D topological materials exhibiting Mexican-hat dispersion. The authors show that this unique band structure significantly enhances plasmon strength and predicts an additional low-frequency plasmon branch when electron-hole symmetry is broken. The results highlight the deep connection between plasmon excitation modes and singularities in the joint density of states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between electronic band structure singularities (like Van Hove points) and collective excitations (plasmons) is a core topic in condensed matter theory, relevant for understanding plasmonic responses in novel 2D materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the unique electronic structure arising from Mexican-hat dispersion (MHD) in 2D topological materials affects the properties of interband plasmons (IBPs).

**Main result.** MHD significantly strengthens the plasmons, reduces their damping, and causes the emergence of an additional, low-frequency plasmon branch when electron-hole symmetry is broken.

**Method.** The analysis is performed using the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) framework, focusing on calculating the polarization function, the joint density of states (JDOS), and the interband overlap function.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D topological material with inverted bands, modeled using the BHZ Hamiltonian. Key features include double-valued isoenergetic contours and a Van Hove singularity.

**Key observables.** Interband plasmon spectrum ($\omega_p(q)$), Joint Density of States ($	ext{JDOS}_q$), and the interband quantum metric.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The hybridization parameter $a$, the e-h asymmetry parameter $d$, and the plasmon wave vector $q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes spatial inversion symmetry and treats spin as a good quantum number; the low-frequency branch analysis is restricted to cases where e-h symmetry is broken.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MHD dispersion in both symmetric and asymmetric cases, and show the calculated plasmon spectra ($\omega_{p1}, \omega_{p2}$) relative to the saddle-point singularity energies ($w_{s1}, w_{s2}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by detailing the general formalism (Hamiltonian, overlap function, JDOS), analyzing the plasmon spectrum in the e-h symmetric case, and finally addressing the effects and emergence of the additional low-frequency branch when e-h symmetry is broken.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional topological materials with Mexican-hat dispersion exhibit numerous nontrivial features, such as double-valued isoenergetic contours in k-space, strong mixing of electron and hole states, a van Hove singularity in the density of states, and specific quantum geometric properties. We find that, due to these features, Mexican-hat dispersion significantly strengthens interband plasmons, reduces their damping, and gives rise to an additional branch of the plasmon spectrum when electron-hole symmetry is broken. The plasmon spectra are limited to a finite range of the wave vector, the boundaries of which depend on the strength of the electron-electron interaction. We also reveal a relationship between the features of the plasmon spectrum and singularities of the joint density of states with a finite plasmon wave vector q, as well as features of the interband quantum metric, which also depend on q.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a continuum model for bilayer graphene moiré systems under uniaxial strain to study topological phase transitions. By analyzing the Hamiltonian, the authors predict that strain can induce a topological transition at a specific stretch factor (s ~ 1.018). This work opens a pathway for strain engineering to realize topological transport and quantum anomalous Hall effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete, analytically tractable framework for engineering topological states (like QAH effects) in 2D materials using external stimuli (strain), which is highly relevant for designing quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To construct an analytical continuum model for uniaxially strained bilayer graphene moiré systems to understand the relationship between structural strain and electronic properties, particularly topological phase transitions.

**Main result.** The model predicts a topological phase transition occurring at a critical stretch factor s ~ 1.018, characterized by a change in the Chern number and detectable via the nonlinear Hall effect.

**Method.** The authors employ a continuum model approach, analogous to the Bistritzer-MacDonald model, to derive and diagonalize the effective Hamiltonian for the moiré superlattice.

**Model / system.** The system is AB-stacked bilayer graphene subjected to uniaxial strain along the x-direction, forming a 1D moiré superlattice (SBLG). The Hamiltonian is constructed by treating interlayer coupling as hopping between several Dirac points.

**Key observables.** Chern number, band gap collapse, topological number inversion, and the nonlinear Hall effect.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stretch factor (s), critical stretch factor (s ~ 1.018), and the hopping amplitudes derived from the strain.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the stretch factor s is small (s < 1.02) and makes approximations when calculating the interlayer coupling term by ignoring interactions with large in-plane separations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural changes due to stretching, the band structure at the critical point showing two degeneracy points along the k_y direction, and the general band structure evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the continuum Hamiltonian, analyzes the low-energy physics near Dirac cones, determines the conditions for band degeneracy points, and finally links these structural changes to topological invariants like the Chern number.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a continuum model for a one-dimensional moiré superlattice formed by stretching one layer of AB-stacked bilayer graphene along the x direction by a factor s. Following the spirit of the Bistritzer-MacDonald model for twisted bilayer graphene, we treat the interlayer coupling as hopping between several Dirac points. At a critical stretch factor s ~ 1.018 the two bands near the Fermi level touch, forming two degeneracy points along the k_y direction. This gap closing is accompanied by a topological phase transition, in which the Chern number changes from 1 to -1, and by a sign change of the Berry-curvature dipole, which we propose can be detected through the nonlinear Hall effect. We find that uniaxial strain modulates inter-Dirac-valley coupling, which drives band gap collapse and subsequent topological number inversion. This opens a route to engineer topological transport and quantum anomalous Hall effects via strain engineering of moiré heterostructures.

</details>
